L9: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
L10: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant, which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L11: Application Type	NME NDA
Application Number(s)	NDA 217779
Priority or Standard	Standard
Submit Date(s)	June 16, 2023
Received Date(s)	June 16, 2023
PDUFA Goal Date	June 16, 2024
Division/Office	Division of Hematologic Malignancies I
Office of Oncologic Diseases
Review Completion Date	May 24, 2024
Established Name	Imetelstat
(Proposed) Trade Name	RYTELO
Pharmacologic Class	Telomerase inhibitor
Code name	GRN163L, JNJ-63935937
Applicant	Geron Corporation
Formulation(s)	Lyophilized powder for intravenous injection
Dosing Regimen	7.1 mg/kg imetelstat (equivalent to 7.5 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) by intravenous infusion over 2 hours every 4 weeks
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	For the treatment of transfusion-dependent anemia in adult patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) who have failed to respond or have lost response to or are ineligible for erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESA)
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Regular approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	For the treatment of adult patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) with transfusion-dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks who have not responded to or have lost response to or are ineligible for erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESA)

L52: ## 1 Executive Summary
L53: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L54: Drug Established Name:	Imetelstat
Trade Name:	Rytelo
Dosage Forms:	Lyophilized powder (47 mg, 188 mg) for intravenous injection
Chemical Class:	Small molecule, oligonucleotide
Therapeutic Class:	Enzyme inhibitor
Mechanism of Action:	Inhibitor of human telomerase
L55: Imetelstat is a covalently-lipidated 13-mer oligonucleotide that binds to the template region of the RNA component of human telomerase (hTR), inhibits telomerase enzymatic activity and prevents telomere binding. Shorter telomere length and high telomerase activity have been reported in lower-risk myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS), suggesting that telomerase may be a relevant therapeutic target for this population.
L56: Imetelstat is being developed for the treatment of anemia in patients with lower-risk MDS and has also been investigated in patients with other hematologic malignancies, including myelofibrosis, and solid tumors. The Applicant submits a New Drug Application (NDA) with results from a randomized phase 3 study 63935937MDS3001 (IMerge, MDS3001) to support the indication “for the treatment of adult patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) with transfusion-dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks who have not responded to or have lost response to or are ineligible for erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESA)” based on an analysis of 8-week red blood cell transfusion independence (RBC-TI). Imetelstat is administered at 7.1 mg/kg (equivalent to imetelstat sodium 7.5 mg/kg) once every 4 weeks by intravenous infusion over 2 hours.
L57: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L58: Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
L59: Information submitted by the Applicant provides substantial evidence for the effectiveness of imetelstat for the treatment of low- to intermediate-1 risk MDS with transfusion dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks after loss of response to ESA treatment. The efficacy of imetelstat has been established primarily on the results of a single randomized, placebo-controlled, multicenter, blinded trial in (Study MDS3001, IMerge).
L60: Support of the efficacy of imetelstat for this indication was provided from the phase 2 part of MDS3001 which preceded phase 3.
L61: In the phase 3 part of MDS3001, patients were randomized (2:1) to receive either imetelstat (N=118) or placebo (N=59). Results of the trial demonstrated that imetelstat increases 8-week and 24-week RBC transfusion independence (RBC-TI) in this population. The primary endpoint was the rate of 8-week RBC-TI demonstrated at 39.8% (95% CI: 30.9, 49.3) with imetelstat treatment versus 15.0% (95% CI: 7.1, 26.6) with placebo treatment (p &lt; 0.001) and the rate of 24-week RBC-TI was 28.0% (95% CI: 20.1, 37.0) with imetelstat treatment versus 3.3% (95% CI: 0.4, 11.5) with placebo treatment (p &lt; 0.001).
L62: However, a statistically significant treatment effect was not observed on other secondary endpoints reflective of a disease-modifying effect, including erythroid response (HI-E) per International Working Group (IWG) 2006 criteria, complete remission or partial remission (CR or PR), and overall survival (OS). In addition, the patient-reported outcomes collected in the Phase 3 study did not reflect an improvement in fatigue or other anemia-related symptoms.
L63: Despite the lack of improvement in efficacy endpoints other than RBC-TI, the demonstration of improvement in 8- and 24-week RBC-TI was determined to outweigh the risks of treatment with imetelstat, and the review team recommends regular approval.
L64: Labeling will describe the primary endpoint of \ge 8-week RBC-TI and the key secondary endpoint of \ge 24-week RBC-TI. Median follow up time was similar between arms (19.5 months with imetelstat versus 17.5 months with placebo) and will also be reported in labeling.
L65: Supportive information was provided from the phase 2 part of MDS3001. The population enrolled in phase 2 was similar but included patients with more prior therapies and allowed prior treatment with lenalidomide and hypomethylating agents (HMAs). Fifty-seven (57) patients were enrolled and the 8-week RBC-TI in this population was 36.8% (95% CI: 24.5, 50.7) for all subjects in the Phase 2 study, which is generally consistent with the 8-week RBC-TI rate observed in the Phase 3 study.
L66: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L67: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L68: MDS is a heterogeneous disorder arising from clonal expansion of a hematopoietic progenitor. This leads to bone marrow dysplasia, ineffective hematopoiesis, and a risk of transformation to AML. Patients with MDS are broadly classified into lower risk and higher risk disease categories based on several factors that impact survival. The median survival is reported to be 5.7 years for low risk and 3.5 years for intermediate-1 risk, with some patients surviving up to 1 to 2 decades. Anemia is the most common manifestation of MDS leading to fatigue, decreased quality of life, and the need for red blood cell transfusion. Initial treatment for anemia due to MDS is typically erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) and few treatments are available for patients who lose response to ESAs.
L69: Study MDS3001-Phase 3 was a randomized, placebo-controlled, multicenter, blinded trial in patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk MDS with transfusion dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks who have failed to respond to, lost response to, or are ineligible for ESA treatment. Patients were treated with imetelstat 7.1 mg/kg IV every 4 weeks, given as a 2-hour infusion, or a matching placebo. The primary endpoint was 8-week RBC transfusion independence. Patients were randomized (2:1) to receive either imetelstat (N=118) or placebo (N=59).
L70: The primary efficacy trial, Study MDS3001-Part 2 (Phase 3), met its primary endpoint of 8-week RBC-TI (39.8% imetelstat versus 15.0% placebo; p &lt; 0.001) in addition to the key secondary endpoint of 24-week RBC-TI (28.0% imetelstat versus 3.3% placebo; p = &lt;0.001). Although the median duration of RBC-TI was only 5.0 weeks (95% CI 4.0, 7.7) for imetelstat versus 3.9 weeks (95% CI 3.6, 4.0) for placebo in all subjects, the median duration of RBC-TI in 8-week RBC-TI responders was 51.6 weeks (95% CI 26.9, 83.9) for imetelstat versus 13.3 weeks (95% 8.0, 24.9) for placebo, indicating that a subset of patients has a clinically meaningful duration of benefit.
L71: However, there was no evidence of a statistically significant treatment effect on the key secondary endpoint of HI-E per IWG 2006 criteria, or on other secondary endpoints reflective of a disease-modifying effect such as CR/PR or OS. Additionally, the patient-reported outcomes collected in the Phase 3 study did not reflect an improvement in fatigue or other anemia-related symptoms. There were also residual
L72: uncertainties regarding the impact of imetelstat on overall medical resource utilization and the applicability of the trial results to the US population.
L73: There are concerns regarding the safety profile, given the high rate of cytopenias including grade 3-4 neutropenia (71% with imetelstat versus 7% with placebo) and thrombocytopenia (65% with imetelstat versus 8% with placebo) observed with imetelstat. Subjects treated with imetelstat also required more interventions for cytopenias, such as myeloid growth factors and platelet transfusions, compared to patients receiving placebo. Thirty-five percent of patients required myeloid growth factor at least once during treatment in the imetelstat arm compared to 2 patients in the placebo arm. Most of those patients required multiple administrations of myeloid growth factor. Eighteen percent of patients on the imetelstat arm required at least one platelet transfusion during treatment, with patients requiring platelet transfusions in up to 10 separate episodes. Despite the increased use of growth factor support for patients who experienced neutropenia in the imetelstat arm, a higher rate of infection was observed in patients who received imetelstat (all grade infections 42% with imetelstat versus 34% with placebo). Similarly, patients receiving imetelstat experienced more hemorrhagic events (all grade hemorrhage 21% with imetelstat versus 12% with placebo). However, the rates of Grade 3-4 infections and hemorrhage were notably similar between the imetelstat and placebo arms.
L74: Given residual uncertainties regarding the benefit-risk profile of imetelstat, FDA held an Oncologic Drugs Advisory Committee (ODAC) meeting on 14 March 2024. The ODAC voted that the potential benefits of imetelstat outweigh the risks. Given this, in addition to the high unmet need for the proposed patient population, FDA has concluded that the benefits of imetelstat outweigh the risks for the treatment of adult patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk MDS with transfusion-dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks who have not responded to or have lost response to or are ineligible for ESA.
L75: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	MDS is a chronic condition with no curative treatment options except hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT)
Lower-risk MDS (LR-MDS) has a long life expectancy and treatment is typically aimed at reducing symptom burden
Transfusion burden due to anemia is a common complication of lower risk MDS and can lead to lower quality of life, iron overload, and other issues
There is an urgent need for new therapeutic options for patients with MDS, including both treatment for the underlying disease and for amelioration of symptoms.
Current Treatment Options	Typical frontline therapy for transfusion-dependent anemia due to LR-MDS includes erythropoiesis stimulating agents (ESAs).
Luspatercept is another option for frontline therapy and was recently FDA approved for use in this setting; it is also indicated for use after ESA failure in patients with anemia due to MDS with ringed sideroblasts (MDS-RS) or myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasms with ringed sideroblasts and thrombocytosis (MDS/MPN-RS-T).
Lenalidomide is approved for the treatment of transfusion-dependent anemia in the subset of patients with LR-MDS with deletion 5q (del5q) and is sometimes used off-label in patients without del5q.
Hypomethylating agents such as azacitidine, decitabine, and decitabine-cedazuridine, are additional therapeutic options, though often reserved for the treatment of high-risk MDS or refractory LR-MDS in clinical practice.
The current treatment options for treating anemia in the context of MDS after ESA failure are inadequate and further options are needed.
Benefit	Study MDS3001-Part 2 (Phase 3) was an international, multi-center, double-blind, randomized (2:1), placebo-controlled trial comparing imetelstat (n=118) vs placebo (n=60) in patients with transfusion-dependent anemia (requiring at least 4 RBC units/8 weeks at baseline)
Imetelstat demonstrated superiority over placebo in Study MDS3001-Part 2 (Phase 3) on the primary endpoint of 8-week RBC-TI and the key secondary endpoint of 24-week RBC-TI. A subset of patients had a clinically meaningful

In the ITT analysis of 8-week RBC-TI, the primary endpoint, the rate difference was 24.8% (95% CI 9.9, 36.9).
In the ITT analysis of 24-week RBC-TI, a key secondary endpoint, the rate difference was 24.6% (95% CI 12.6, 34.2).
The median duration of response was 51.6 weeks (95% CI 26.9, 83.9) for 8-week RBC-TI responders, suggesting a clinically meaningful duration of benefit for a subset of patients. However, the median duration of response was only 5.0 weeks (95% CI 4.0, 7.7) when considering all subjects treated with imetelstat.
Analyses of 8-week RBC-IT over subgroups showed a consistent treatment effect favoring imetelstat over placebo in most subgroups tested. The rate was higher with placebo in the subgroup of patients with prior luspatercept use but the number of patients in this subgroup was too few to draw conclusions. The treatment effect was marginal for subjects with lower transfusion burden at baseline (defined as 4-7 RBC units in 16 weeks in at least 2 transfusion episodes).

L77: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and Risk Management	Imetelstat was associated with a higher risk of Grade 3+ AEs, SAEs, and AEs leading to treatment modification. However, the rate of fatal AEs is similar to placebo.
Imetelstat was associated with a high risk of cytopenias with resultant need for intervention and increased risk of all grade infection and hemorrhage. However, the rates of Grade 3-4 infection and hemorrhage were similar to placebo.
Infusion-related reactions occurred in 8% of patients with MDS treated with imetelstat; Grade 3-4 infusion-related reactions occurred in 1.7%.
Imetelstat was associated with a higher risk of fractures, arthralgias/myalgias, and bone pain.
Although patient-reported deterioration in fatigue for at least 2 consecutive fatigue assessments (as measured by FACIT-Fatigue) was similar between arms, investigator-reported all grade fatigue was slightly higher in the imetelstat arm.
A QT assessment has not been completed.
Lower dosages of imetelstat have not been evaluated in patients with LR-MDS.
Serious toxicities were mitigated in the clinical trials by use of premedications, frequent monitoring for known toxicities, and dose modifications.
Imetelstat has increased toxicities compared to placebo, including cytopenias and tolerability issues. However, the rates of Grade 3-4 infection and hemorrhage with imetelstat are similar to placebo, suggesting that serious consequences of cytopenias are able to be mitigated. Per the Oncology Drug Advisory Committee discussion on 14 March 2024, the safety profile of imetelstat is acceptable for patients with transfusion-dependent anemia due to LR-MDS.
Further characterizations of safety, optimal dosage, pharmacokinetics, and effects on QT are needed. A patient medication guide is required to inform and educate patients of the risks of thrombocytopenia and neutropenia and when to seek immediate medical attention. Labeling should include warnings for the serious risks of thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, infusion-related reactions, and embryofetal toxicity, and instructions for monitoring and dose modifications for toxicities.

L86: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L87: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L88: ## The Applicant's Position:
L89: Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) are serious and life threatening and are characterized by ineffective hemopoiesis, which manifests in abnormal dysplastic cell morphology in one or more hematopoietic cell lines and the development of peripheral cytopenias (Foran and Shammo, 2012; Adès, 2014; Bejar and Steensma, 2014). Myelodysplastic syndromes are one of the most common hematologic malignancies in older people, with a median age at diagnosis of over 70 years old and less than 10% of patients being younger than 50 years old (Neukirchen, 2011; Fenaux, 2021). Lower-risk (LR) MDS are characterized by the presence of bone marrow (BM) dysplasia, low BM blast percentage, low number and depth of cytopenia(s), and a variety of karyotypic and molecular abnormalities (Carraway and Saygin, 2020). While LR MDS patients generally have a lower risk of progression to acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and an overall better prognosis, there are certain features in LR MDS that could result in higher-risk disease, including presence and number of cytopenias and certain mutations (DeZern and Dalton, 2022), as well as short telomeres and high telomerase activity (Mittelman, 2010). Median survival in LR MDS ranges from 32.0 months to 56.1 months (Zeidan, 2019). Infection is the most common cause of death in LR MDS, representing more than one-third of all disease-related deaths; leukemic transformation and (mostly intracranial or gastrointestinal) bleeding complications are the cause of death in approximately 15% and 13% of LR MDS patients, respectively (Zeidan, 2019).
L90: Approximately 40% of LR MDS patients develop anemia and become transfusion dependent, which together with lower hemoglobin (Hgb) levels have a significant negative correlation with survival and are associated with an increased likelihood of progression to AML (Malcovati, 2005; Platzbecker, 2012; Hellstrom-Lindberg and van de Loosdrecht, 2013; Zeidan, 2013; Adès, 2014; Fenaux, 2021). Patients with chronic transfusion dependent anemia are highly symptomatic and suffer from exhaustive fatigue, shortness of breath and palpitations resulting in an impaired quality of life (QoL) and most importantly, shortened overall survival (OS). Over time patients develop end organ dysfunction not only due to chronic anemia but also because of iron overload from numerous and frequent transfusions (Singhal, 2017; Germing, 2019; de Swart, 2020; Platzbecker, 2021). In addition, long-term red blood cell (RBC) transfusion dependence has other clinical and economic consequences, including a potentially negative impact on health related QoL, iron overload and its associated end organ complications, higher risk of cardiovascular complications, and increased risk of infections (Platzbecker, 2021; Volpe, 2022). Furthermore, a higher rate of RBC transfusion has been shown to correlate significantly
L91: with decreased progression free survival (PFS) and OS and achieving transfusion independence (TI) with treatment has been significantly associated with prolonged OS in multiple studies and a meta-analysis (De Witte, 2020; Braga Lemos, 2021; Santini, 2022). Therefore, achieving TI for LR MDS patients represents an important clinical benefit that can have a substantial impact on day-to-day functioning and longer-term clinical outcomes such as improved survival rates and lower risk of disease progression.
L92: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L93: Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) are a heterogeneous group of disorders characterized by anemia, ineffective erythropoiesis, dysplastic changes in hematologic cells, and varying degrees of neutropenia and thrombocytopenia. It is a rare disease, with an incidence in the United States of 3.8 per 100,000 estimated for 2019. It is more common in men and is more common in older patients, with an incidence of 13.0 in people aged 65-74 and 39.7 for people aged 75 and older. Some ethnic variation is observed, with the highest incidence in non-Hispanic Whites at 4.2, lowest incidence in Asians at 2.4, and intermediate risk for Blacks, Hispanic/Latinos, and American Indian/Alaskan Natives (SEER Cancer Statistics Explorer Network, SEER*Explorer, updated June 8, 2023, accessed August 16, 2023).
L94: Various systems for characterizing the risk of MDS have been proposed. One commonly used system is the International Prognosis Scoring System (IPSS) (Greenberg 1997), updated to the Revised International Prognosis Scoring System (IPSS-R) in 2012 (Greenberg 2012) and then to the Molecular International Prognosis Scoring System (IPSS-M) in 2022 (Bernard 2022). The IPSS utilizes bone marrow blast percentage, karyotype, and number of cytopenias to divide MDS into four risk categories (low, intermediate-1, intermediate-2, and high). The IPSS-R takes each cell line (red blood cells, platelets, and neutrophils) into account separately and uses five prognostic categories from very low to very high risk. The IPSS-M incorporates molecular features to divide MDS into 6 risk categories (very low, low, moderate low, moderate high, high, very high risk). Historically, patients with IPSS low and intermediate or IPSS-R very low, low, and intermediate risk have been considered to be lower risk, although patients with IPSS-R intermediate risk may be considered higher risk depending on their blast count and other variables.
L95: Patients with MDS have a variable prognosis, but are at increased risk of infection, bleeding, and complications of anemia. In addition, MDS may transform into acute myeloid leukemia (AML). AML that occurs as transformation from MDS is resistant to therapy and has a poorer prognosis than de novo AML. Patients with IPSS low and intermediate-1 disease have a median survival of 5.7 and 3.5 years, respectively (Greenberg 1997). The risk of transformation to AML is low for patients with IPSS low MDS and is about 6% per year for patients with intermediate-1 MDS. Similarly, patients with IPSS-R very low, low, and intermediate risk MDS have a median survival of 8.8, 5.3, and 3.0 years, respectively. Twenty-five percent of patients with IPSS-R low
L96: and intermediate disease will transform to AML in 10.8 and 3.2 years, respectively (Greenberg 2012).
L97: Patients with MDS often require red blood cell (RBC) or platelet transfusion due to the anemia that is associated with MDS. As noted above, the requirement for RBC transfusion has a deleterious effect on patient survival and quality of life (QOL) (Fenaux 2021). Patients who require RBC transfusions to treat or ameliorate MDS risk iron overload, require frequent clinic visits for monitoring and transfusion, and may not have complete resolution of symptoms associated with anemia. However, it is not clear that improving anemia or reducing transfusion burden is associated with an improved survival or quality of life. A meta-analysis of QOL evaluations in patients with MDS treated for anemia found no clear relationship between QOL and treatment of anemia (Mo 2023). Specific issues regarding issues surrounding various methods of reducing transfusion burden with specific agents will be discussed below.
L98: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L99: ## The Applicant's Position:
L100: The number of approved therapies for all risk categories of MDS is limited, and not all agents have been shown to be highly efficacious or improve survival. The proposed indication for imetelstat is transfusion-dependent anemia in adult patients with low- to intermediate-1 risk MDS who have failed to respond or have lost response to or are ineligible for erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESA).
L101: Currently there are no approved treatments that are inclusive of this patient population, who as a result have an unanswered and high unmet need as outlined in Applicant Table 1.
L102: **Applicant Table 1: Current Treatments for LR MDS**
L103: Therapies for LR MDSa	RS- and RS+	Non del(5q)	IPSS low/Int-1
Luspatercept	No (only RS+)	Yes	Yes
Lenalidomide	Yes	No	Yes
HMA (including DEC-Cb)	Yes	Yes	Nob
L104: DEC-C = decitabine and cedazuridine; HMA = hypomethylating agent; Int-1 = intermediate-1; IPSS = International Prognostic Scoring system; LR MDS = lower-risk MDS; RS+/- = with or without ring sideroblasts.
L105: a Differential treatment patterns across the US population by race, ethnicity, sex or age have not been identified to date.
L106: b DEC-C is approved in patients with IPSS intermediate- 1 MDS; however, it is not recommended in LR MDS unless following azacitidine or decitabine failure.
L107: Currently available treatments for LR MDS include:
L108: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L109: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L110: - Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents have been well established as first-line treatment of anemia in LR MDS. However, patients with LR MDS without del(5q) who have failed ESA treatment or do not respond or have short response to ESAs are at a higher risk of transformation to AML and poorer survival [median 36.7 vs. 54.3 months in patients with longer response to ESA; (Kelaidi, 2013)].
- Lenalidomide is approved as a treatment of transfusion-dependent anemia due to LR MDS associated with del(5q). The del(5q) mutation is observed in 10% to 15% of patients with MDS. Lenalidomide is not currently approved to treat MDS patients who are non-del(5q). The sub-set of transfusion dependent patients with non-del(5q) MDS who are treated with lenalidomide do not respond or have TI of short duration.
- Hypomethylating agents have been used in LR MDS patients, but they are primarily used in patients with higher risk MDS. In LR MDS, they have not been shown to modify the natural history of the disease or prolong OS in patients with LR MDS (Garcia-Manero, 2014; Wan and Han, 2021). Given the limited benefit observed in LR MDS, hypomethylating agents (HMA) are generally not recommended as standard of care for this patient population (NCCN, 2022). Although decitabine and cedazuridine (DEC-C) is approved in the US for treatment of MDS with intermediate-1 risk, the National Comprehensive Cancer Network recommends DEC-C for treating higher-risk patients as an alternative to azacitidine or decitabine or as a bridge to HSCT (NCCN, 2022).
- Luspatercept is approved for the treatment of anemia failing and ESA and requiring \ge 2 RBC units over 8 weeks in MDS-RS+ patients. The 8-week TI rate is 38% with median duration of response of 30.6 weeks (Fenaux, 2020). However, benefit was shown mostly in patients with a lower transfusion burden and coupled with the restriction to MDS-RS+ patients, which are less likely to progress to AML compared with the larger pool of MDS-RS- patients, this leaves a large proportion of the transfusion-dependent LR MDS patients still underserved.
L111: In summary, to date, there is no therapy for the treatment of transfusion dependent anemia in LR MDS who have failed to respond or have lost response or are ineligible to ESA that has shown sustained and durable activity in a broad patient population that alleviates the core signs and symptoms of transfusion dependency in a continuous manner.
L112: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L113: There are few treatment options available for patients with MDS. Approved treatments for MDS are limited to hypomethylating agents (decitabine, azacitidine, decitabine-cedazuridine) and lenalidomide for patients with del 5q. Luspatercept was recently approved for treatment of anemia in ESA-naïve patients with IPSS-R very low- to intermediate-risk MDS who may require regular RBC transfusions. Luspatercept was also previously approved for the treatment of anemia failing an ESA and requiring 2 or more RBC units over 8 weeks in adults with very low-
L114: to intermediate-risk MDS with ringed sideroblasts or with myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasm with ring sideroblasts and thrombocytosis. ESA are often used for treatment of anemia in MDS but are not approved for use in MDS and do not treat the underlying disease.
L115: The only potentially curative option is hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). However, HSCT has been associated with lower survival in patients with lower risk MDS and is not recommended for patients with lower risk disease (Koreth 2013; Cutler 2004).
L116: The hypomethylating agents (HMAs) azacitidine and decitabine are approved for use in MDS. The approval for azacitidine does not specify IPSS or IPSS-R score (Vidaza USPI). Decitabine and oral decitabine/cedazuridine are approved for use in MDS with the following French-American-British subtypes (refractory anemia, refractory anemia with ringed sideroblasts, refractory anemia with excess blasts, and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia) and intermediate-1, intermediate-2, and high risk IPSS groups (Dacogen USPI and Inqovi USPI). In practice, use of HMAs is frequently restricted to higher risk MDS. However, one study examining the use of azacitidine in patients with IPSS low or intermediate-1 risk disease who were transfusion dependent and ESA resistant showed hematologic improvement and increased transfusion independence in patients who received azacitidine compared to best supportive care (Sanchez-Garcia 2018). Studies using decitabine in lower risk MDS have demonstrated better response rates compared to supportive care but have mixed results concerning survival and event-free survival (Ye 2021).
L117: Anemia is a major symptom for patients with MDS and may worsen concurrent medical issues such as arterial vascular disease. As noted above, transfusion dependence is a poor prognostic sign for patients with MDS. However, it is not clear that achieving transfusion independence with treatment leads to improved survival or quality of life (Garcia-Manero 2023). One retrospective study suggested longer overall survival in patients with lower risk MDS treated with ESAs (Park2008). However, a prospective trial comparing ESA to supportive care found no statistically significant overall survival benefit (Greenberg 2009). Treatment with oral azacitidine (Garcia-Manero2021) has been associated with a higher risk of death early in treatment and luspatercept (Clinical Review for luspatercept BLA, Original-2, 2019) has been associated with a higher risk of progression to AML and second primary malignancies. Treatment of patients with 5q- MDS was associated with a higher rate of transfusion independence compared to placebo, but no significant increase in survival or decrease in risk of transformation to AML were reported (Fenaux 2011). As noted above, one study of azacitidine in lower risk disease showed higher rates of TI compared to supportive care only. However, this trial did not demonstrate any change in leukemia-free survival (Sanchez-Garcia 2018).
L118: Trials reporting quality of life (QOL) outcomes in patients with lower risk MDS with transfusion dependence treated with a variety of agents have not consistently shown improvement in QOL
L119: outcomes. Some studies (Sanchez-Garcia 2018; Santini 2016; Greenberg 2009) have shown no or minimal improvement in quality of life among patients on active treatment in clinical trials, although it should be noted that patients who respond may have improved QOL by some measures (Santini 2016; Greenberg 2009). Analysis of QOL data in patients treated with luspatercept showed a decrease in QOL (Clinical Review for luspatercept BLA, Original-2, 2019).
L120: Trials using a variety of agents to treat MDS or anemia in MDS have not consistently demonstrated that a decrease in transfusion dependence results in better outcomes for patients overall. Thus, it cannot be assumed that decreased transfusion burden or TI will result in either a survival benefit or improved quality of life. Transfusion independence must be supported by evidence of improvement in clinical outcomes to ensure that the patient is benefiting from treatment.
L121: ## 3 Regulatory Background

Imetelstat was granted Orphan Drug Designation for the treatment of MDS in 2015, as well as Fast Track Designation for MDS in 2017. Geron currently has two ongoing Phase 3 studies, one in MDS and the other in MF, (b) (4)

L148: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L149: The following information is summarized from the Product Quality Assessment.
L150: Imetelstat sodium is a sodium salt of a 13-mer oligonucleotide N3'→P5' thio-phosphoramidate conjugated with a lipid palmitoyl group attached to the 5' terminus through an aminoglycerol linker. The structure of imetelstat sodium includes thiophosphoramidate linkages, with sulfuration of the native phosphodiester, and replacement of the 3'-oxygen to provide a 3'-amino group.

L159: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L160: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L161: Imetelstat is a 13-mer thio-phosphoramidate oligonucleotide telomerase inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adult patients with low- to intermediate 1 risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) with transfusion-dependent anemia requiring 4 or more red blood cell units over 8 weeks who have not responded to or have lost response to or are ineligible for erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESA). It binds to the template region of the RNA component of human telomerase (hTR), which lies in the catalytic site of human telomerase reverse transcriptase (hTERT), resulting in competitive inhibition of hTERT enzymatic activity. The nonclinical development program for imetelstat included various in vitro and in vivo studies to evaluate the pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, general toxicology, reproductive and developmental effects, and the genotoxic potential of imetelstat.
L162: In vitro studies have shown that imetelstat inhibited telomerase activity in the HT-3 human cervical carcinoma cell line in a concentration-dependent manner with an IC_{50} of 0.056 \muM. The in vivo activity of imetelstat was assessed using multiple mouse models. A single or repeated administration of imetelstat in mice resulted in telomerase inhibition in the liver tissues collected for activity analysis. The anti-tumor activity of imetelstat was evaluated in the OVCAR-5 human ovarian cancer xenograft model. The animals were administered imetelstat intraperitoneally (IP) at 15 or 30 mg/kg three times per week (TIW) for 50 days. The results showed tumor growth inhibition with an average reduction in tumor volume of 34% and 53%, respectively, at 15 and 30 mg/kg. No xenograft studies were conducted in MDS cells lines, and this was described by the Applicant as being due to the lack of MDS specific cell lines and mouse xenograft models.
L163: In the safety pharmacology study conducted in monkeys (Study No. GS04-072), 4 male animals were assigned to a single treatment group. The animals were treated with vehicle control, 5 mg/kg, 10 mg/kg, and 15 mg/kg of imetelstat from Days 1-28 via IV infusion. Each animal received a dose of vehicle control on Day 1 and increasing doses of imetelstat on Days 2, 9, 21 and 28. There were no test article-related changes observed in the cardiovascular, central nervous or respiratory systems. Consistent with these findings, the electrocardiogram (ECG) assessments evaluated in the 9-month toxicology study in monkeys were considered normal and no test article-related changes were observed.
L164: The plasma exposures of imetelstat increased in a dose-dependent manner in mice without showing a sex-difference. In monkeys, increases in plasma exposure were slightly greater than
L165: 40
L166: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L167: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L168: dose proportional and higher exposures in male compared to female monkeys were observed. Distribution studies demonstrated high (&gt; 90%) plasma protein binding of imetelstat across species (human, monkey, rat, and mouse). The highest concentration of imetelstat was seen in the liver and kidney, followed by the bone marrow and spleen. An excretion study in rats showed that the primary route of elimination was urine with approximately 62% to 82% of the dose recovered by 168 hours post-dose.
L169: Repeat-dose studies in mice and monkeys were conducted to assess the general toxicology of imetelstat. The drug was dosed for 6 months in rats and 9 months in monkeys via IV administration, which is consistent with the intended clinical route of administration. Overall, adverse findings included activation of the alternative complement pathway, thrombocytopenia, altered coagulation, and toxicities in the liver and kidney. In the 6-month mouse study, imetelstat was administered at dose levels of 5, 15, or 20 mg/kg by IV bolus injection twice a week (BIW) with an 8-week recovery period. A total of 16 mortalities occurred including 4 animals in the control group. The cause of the deaths was either considered unrelated to imetelstat or could not be determined. Local reaction of tail discoloration was noted at the injection site, which correlated with microscopic observations of necrosis/inflammation and vascular/perivascular fibrosis. Decreased RBC parameters and extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen were observed. Increased liver, kidney and spleen weights were also noted. Microscopic findings in the liver consisted of increases in inflammatory foci and Kupffer cells, and/or histiocytic aggregates. In the 9-month monkey study, imetelstat was administered at doses of 5, 10, or 15 mg/kg via 2-hour IV infusion once weekly with an 8-week recovery period. There was no imetelstat-related mortality. Imetelstat-related findings included decreases in RBC parameters and platelet counts, and erythroid hyperplasia evident based on the evaluation of bone marrow smears. Prolongation of activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) and activation of alternative complement pathways (Bb, C5a) were observed. Increased liver and kidney weights correlated with microscopic changes in the kidney (glomerulosclerosis, tubular hemorrhage, and protein casts) and liver (capsular telangiectasis and brown pigment deposition in Kupffer cells). Other toxicities occurred in the uterus (endometrium atrophy), brain (mononuclear cell infiltration in the choroid plexus) and spleen (enlarged).
L170: In a pivotal embryo-fetal development (EFD) study in mice, imetelstat was administered to pregnant mice via slow-bolus IV injection on gestation day (GD) 6, 9, and 12 at doses of 4.7, 14.1 or 28.2 mg/kg (equivalent to 0, 5, 15 or 30 mg/kg imetelstat sodium). In a dose range-finding (DRF) study in mice, increased rates of post-implantation loss and early resorptions were observed at the high dose of 28.2 mg/kg (equivalent to 30 mg/kg imetelstat sodium). The exposure (based on AUC) at 28.2 mg/kg was 2.5 times the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose of 7.1 mg/kg. In a pivotal EFD study in rabbits, imetelstat was administered to pregnant rabbits by 2-hour IV infusion on GD 6 and 13, at doses of 4.7, 14.1 or
L171: 28.2 mg/kg (equivalent to 5, 15 or 30 mg/kg imetelstat sodium). Increased rates of post-implantation loss and early resorptions that resulted in a decreased number of viable fetuses and litter size per animal, and decreased uterine weight were observed at \ge 14.1 mg/kg. The high dose of 28.2 mg/kg was maternally toxic due to mortality. The mid dose of 14.1 mg/kg had exposure that was 9.3 times the exposure at the recommended clinical dose based on the AUC values.
L172: In genetic toxicity studies, imetelstat was not mutagenic in the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay. The drug was also negative for clastogenicity in both the in vitro micronucleus assay in human lymphocyte cultures and the in vivo mouse bone marrow micronucleus assay. The highest dose tested in the in vivo mouse assay was the maximum-tolerated dose (103.6 mg/kg) of imetelstat.
L173: The need for carcinogenicity studies depends on the life expectancy of the patients for the proposed indications. Based on the literature provided by the Applicant and discussion with the clinical team, the life expectancy for patients with lower risk MDS (defined variously, but usually as low or intermediate-1 risk disease by IPSS) and ESA failure or transfusion dependency is &gt; 5 years and carcinogenicity risk assessment is warranted for the intended patient population. To address the carcinogenicity risk assessment of imetelstat, there will be separate Post Marketing Requirements (PMRs) for carcinogenicity assessments in the mouse and rat species.
L174: With regard to the label, the proposed established pharmacological class (EPC) for imetelstat is “oligonucleotide telomerase inhibitor”. The structural information of the drug (oligonucleotide) has been added to the EPC to better describe the drug class. Additionally, a Warning and Precaution for Embryo-Fetal Toxicity has been added based on the findings in the EFD toxicity studies and the need for contraception. The recommendation for the duration of contraception of 1 week follows the recommendations described in the FDA guidance *Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations*.
L175: This NDA is approvable from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.

L177: ## The Applicant's Position:
L178: Imetelstat is a New Molecular Entity (NME) and there are no referenced NDAs, Biologics Licence Applications (BLAs), or Drug Master Files (DMFs) related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology data for imetelstat.
L179: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L180: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant's Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L181: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L182: ## 5.3.1. Primary pharmacology
L183: ## The Applicant's Position:
L184: Telomerase is highly expressed and activated in most cancers and neoplastic progenitor cells while generally undetectable in normal somatic cells, making it a rational target for cancer therapy (Harley, 2008). Imetelstat is a first in class telomerase inhibitor that specifically targets malignant cells that are characterized by the abnormal elevation of telomerase activity (TA). The primary pharmacology of imetelstat has been evaluated in various cancer cell lines in vitro, and in malignant hematopoietic stem/progenitor cells (HPCs/HSCs) and leukemia stem cells (LSCs) from primary patient samples ex vivo. These studies demonstrate that imetelstat inhibits TA in a dose-dependent manner, leading to loss of a cancer cell's ability to maintain telomere length, and resulting in inhibition of cell proliferation, reduction in colony formation and induction of apoptosis/senescence (Herbert 2005, Shammas 2008, Brennan 2010, Mosoyan 2017, Wang 2018, Baerlocher 2019). In addition, imetelstat has shown anti-tumor activity in vivo in xenograft models of relevant myeloid malignancies (Study PRE-14-004, Bruedigam 2014, Ma 2017, Wang 2018).
L185: Due to the lack of MDS specific cell lines and mouse xenograft models and given the increased expression of telomerase across most cancer types, particularly myeloid malignancies that are closely related to MDS, nonclinical data were generated in models of other tumor types that are relevant to demonstrate imetelstat's mechanism of action and antitumor activity.
L186: Dose-dependent inhibition of TA by imetelstat was demonstrated in vitro in multiple cancer cell lines of different tumor types treated with 0.1–10 µM of imetelstat in vitro for 24 hours to evaluate TA level by Telomerase Repeated Amplification Protocol. The half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) values ranged from 0.15 to 1.35 µM (Herbert, 2005). These data confirm the mechanism-of-action based pharmacodynamic activity of imetelstat in vitro, at clinically achievable concentrations.
L187: The relationship of plasma concentration, TA inhibition and antitumor efficacy was evaluated across in vivo mouse models to understand the optimal pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of imetelstat needed for anti-tumor efficacy. The IP administration of 30 mg/kg imetelstat TIW resulted in plasma concentrations of the drug in mice (71.5 µg/mL; Study P0006-2010) comparable to clinically achievable exposures (peak plasma concentration [C_{max}] of 89.2 µg/mL in MDS patients), and resulted in ≥ 50% inhibition of TA in tumor and surrogate tissues (Studies GS03-047 and GS03-034), leading to significant tumor growth inhibition (TGI) (53%) in xenograft models (Study 4D Protocol 0017-2009). The effects on TGI were usually seen after 3 to 4 weeks of treatment on a TIW schedule at a minimum dose of 15 mg/kg in nude mice. These results
L188: suggest that \ge 50\% inhibition of TA is the mechanism-of-action-based optimal PD effect of imetelstat needed for anti-tumor efficacy, and that the imetelstat exposure levels required to achieve such pharmacodynamic activity are achievable in patients.
L189: Myelodysplastic Syndromes are a myeloid malignancy characterized by clonal myeloproliferation arising from malignant HSCs/HPCs with a high level of TA (Briatore, 2009). The anti-malignant HSCs/HPCs and LSCs activity of imetelstat was evaluated using models of other relevant myeloid malignancies including essential thrombocythemia (ET), MF, chronic myeloid leukemia (CML) and AML. Imetelstat at clinically relevant concentrations demonstrated selective activity on growth inhibition and cell death/apoptosis induction in HSCs/HPCs or LSCs in primary samples from ET (Applicant Figure 1) (Baerlocher, 2019), MF (Mosoyan, 2017; Wang, 2018), CML (Ma, 2017) and AML (Study PRE-14-003) patients ex vivo, while it had minimal effects on normal HSCs/HPCs.
L190: **Applicant Figure 1: Differential Effects of Imetelstat on Megakaryocytes Growth**
L191: Dose-response analysis of colony-forming units (CFU) of megakaryocytes (MK) growth from mononuclear cells of ET patients compared with that of healthy individuals at different clinically relevant concentrations of imetelstat (Baerlocher, 2019).
L192: In vivo anti-tumor activity of imetelstat was also evaluated using models of myeloid malignancies, including AML, CML or MF patient sample-derived xenograft (PDX) models. As an example, in one study NSGS immunodeficient mice were injected with primary AML cells isolated from adult patients, and then treated with 15 mg/kg of imetelstat, intraperitoneally (IP), three times a week (TIW). Imetelstat caused significant inhibition of AML engraftment and outgrowth, leading to prolonged OS compared to vehicle control (Applicant Figure 2) (Bruedigam, 2014). Additionally, study PRE-14-004 showed that imetelstat significantly reduced the LSC population in the bone marrow of the pediatric AML PDX mice and inhibited self-renewal capacity of LSC by significantly delaying engraftment in mice and improving
L193: survival in the secondary transplantation. Similar results with imetelstat treatment were also observed in CML and MF PDX models (Ma, 2017; Wang, 2018).
L194: ## **Applicant Figure 2: Effects of Imetelstat on Human Acute Myeloid Leukemia Stem Cells Function and Survival in Primary Acute Myeloid Leukemia xenografts**
L195: A) Donor chimerism (hCD45%); B) survival analysis of tertiary human MLL-AF9 AML patient (AML X):NSGS transplants treated with telomerase inhibitor imetelstat or vehicle control. N = 9; two independent experiments. Statistical differences in survival were calculated by Mantel-Cox test. Adapted from (Bruedigam, 2014).
L196: Collectively, these nonclinical data provide evidence that imetelstat reduces/eliminates the malignant HSCs/HPCs and LSCs in vitro/ex vivo and in vivo in models of myeloid malignancies. This is particularly relevant to the proposed indicated MDS patient population, as malignant HSCs are the disease-initiating cells in MDS (Pang, 2013), and malignant HPCs have a reduced ability to differentiate, thus leading to cytopenias, particularly anemia, due to ineffective hematopoiesis (Foran and Shammo, 2012). Elimination of such malignant HPCs may facilitate restoration of normal hematopoiesis in these patients.
L197: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L198: The FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's statements. Only the pharmacology studies conducted by the Applicant or data for which the Applicant has the right of reference are used in this review to support the NDA. Additional details and clarifications regarding the studies are provided in the review below.
L199: An in vitro study has shown that imetelstat inhibited telomerase activity in the HT-3 human cervical carcinoma cell line in a concentration-dependent manner with an IC_{50} of 0.056 \mu M (Study No. GR12-023). A study using mouse passaged pediatric AML patient-derived xenograft (PDX) lines were conducted to assess the effect of imetelstat on leukemia stem cells (Study No. PRE-14-003). The cells were treated ex vivo with imetelstat or mismatch oligonucleotide at final concentrations of 0, 1, 2.5, 5, 10, or 20 \mu M for 48 or 96 hours. The results showed that imetelstat increased the cell death compared to the mismatch oligonucleotide.
L200: In an in vivo study, repeated doses of imetelstat were administered IP at 6.15, 18.45, 36.9, or 55.35 mg/kg TIW for a total of 5 or 9 doses in non-tumor-bearing NOD.scid mice (Study No. GS03-047). Liver tissues were collected for telomerase inhibition analysis. The results showed \ge 50\% of telomerase inhibition in all groups that received 9 doses of imetelstat. In the groups that received 5 doses of imetelstat, doses at \ge 18.45 \text{ mg/kg} resulted in \ge 50\% of telomerase inhibition. A single IV administration of imetelstat (36.9 mg/kg) in BALB/c mice resulted in \ge 50\% telomerase inhibition in liver tissue at 24 hours post-dose and persisted up to 168 hours post-dose (Study no. GS03-034).
L201: The effect of imetelstat was evaluated in the OVCAR-5 human ovarian cancer subcutaneous (SC) xenograft model in Hsd nu/nu mice (Study No. 4D Protocol 0017-2009). The animals were administered imetelstat IP at 15 or 30 mg/kg TIW for 50 days. The results showed tumor growth inhibition with an average reduction in tumor volume of 34% and 53%, respectively, at 15 and 30 mg/kg.
L202: ## 5.3.2. **Secondary Pharmacology**
L203: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L204: Due to self-renewal capacity, a fraction of human normal HPCs express telomerase to support proliferative capability for replacing mature blood cells continuously throughout life (Hohaus, 1997). Although TA is tightly regulated during hematopoiesis, presence of TA in normal HPCs indicates a potential mechanism of action to explain clinical adverse events observed with imetelstat, such as thrombocytopenia. Studies GRN01 and GRN05 evaluated the effect of imetelstat on normal HPCs using in vitro colony forming assays and showed that imetelstat at 3.75, 7.5 and 15 \mu M only impacts colony formation of normal megakaryocytic progenitors (CFU-MK) but not normal erythroid or myeloid progenitors. Furthermore, published results indicated
L205: that imetelstat selectively suppressed spontaneous growth of malignant CFU-MK from patients with ET in a dose-dependent manner, while having no effect on clonogenicity of normal MK progenitors (Applicant Figure 1) (Baerlocher, 2019).
L206: Using an *ex vivo* megakaryopoiesis system, Mosoyan 2017 showed that imetelstat affected normal megakaryopoiesis solely by delaying MK precursor maturation, and such effect was reversible upon drug withdrawal (Mosoyan, 2017). This may account for one of the potential mechanisms of imetelstat-induced, reversible thrombocytopenia in patients. In contrast, imetelstat has more global effects on several stages along the hierarchy of malignant megakaryopoiesis, including selectively decreasing clonogenicity, preventing MK maturation and reducing the secretion of fibrogenic factors. These data provide potential mechanisms by which imetelstat exerts its inhibitory effects on malignant MK.
L207: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L208: The FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's statements. Only the pharmacology studies that the Applicant conducted or has the right of reference are used in this review to support the NDA.
L209: The effects of imetelstat on human erythroid, myeloid and megakaryocyte (MK) progenitor proliferation were evaluated by in vitro colony forming assays (Study No. GRN01). Normal human bone marrow cells were cultured and treated with 0, 0.9, 1.875, 3.75, 7.5 or 15 \muM of imetelstat. Colony formation of erythroid, myeloid, and multipotential progenitors were assessed after 14 days and clonogenic progenitors of the megakaryocytic were assessed after 10-12 days. Imetelstat did not inhibit erythroid or myeloid colony number (although colony morphology was compromised at 15 \muM), but significantly inhibited the number of MK colony growth at \ge 3.75 \muM.
L210: A study was conducted to further investigate the effect of imetelstat on MK colony growth using the same method and imetelstat concentrations as described above (Study No. GRN05). Treatment with \ge 7.5 \muM imetelstat resulted in an increase in the number of small MK colonies (each containing 2-20 cells), representing more mature MK progenitor cells and a decrease in the number of large MK colonies (each contains &gt; 50 cells), which resemble the progeny of the most primitive MK progenitor cells with a high proliferative potential.
L211: ## 5.3.3. Safety Pharmacology
L212: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L213: The safety pharmacology of imetelstat was evaluated in vitro and in vivo (Applicant Table 3).
L214: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L215: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

L218: Abbreviations: GLP = good laboratory practice; hERG = human ether-a-go-go-related gene; HEK = human embryonic kidney; µM = micromolar; IV = Intravenous; NA = not applicable; IC50 = concentration resulting in 50% inhibition; CNS = central nervous system; ECG = electrocardiogram.
L219: Results from two in vitro studies (007-TOX-2007 and PRE-14-002) to evaluate imetelstat effects on the human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) ion channel were consistent in demonstrating that the IC50 was not determined since there was no inhibition effect of imetelstat on hERG currents at concentrations up to 750 mg/mL, which results in a safety margin for imetelstat at least &gt; 140 x higher than the human free plasma concentration at Cmax (~5.35 µg/mL) based on population PK (popPK) predicted Cmax of 89.2 µg/mL for the MDS population following a 7.1 mg/kg dose (equivalent to 7.5 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) and fraction unbound of 0.06 (human plasma protein binding of 94%). In these studies, slight increases in hERG current were observed at 50, 250 and 750 µg/mL of imetelstat. The agonist effect was not dose dependent,
L220: and it may not be clinically relevant as it occurred at a concentration &gt; 46 x higher than free plasma concentrations of imetelstat predicted in MDS patients.
L221: In the safety pharmacology study in telemetered cynomolgus monkeys (Study GS04-072), no significant changes in mean arterial pressure, heart rate, respiratory rate, body temperature, electrocardiographic activity, or blood gas parameters were observed up to the highest dose tested (15 mg/kg) at imetelstat plasma concentration levels &gt; 2.6 x higher than imetelstat C_{max} in subjects with MDS. Consistent with these findings, there were no imetelstat-related changes observed in electrocardiography, blood pressure, and respiratory rate at dose levels up to 15 mg/kg/week in the chronic toxicity study in monkeys (002-TOX-2008).
L222: Overall, imetelstat is considered to have very low likelihood of presenting any safety risk for the cardiovascular, central nervous system (CNS) and respiratory systems.
L223: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L224: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's summary for safety pharmacology. Additional details and clarification regarding the safety pharmacology assessments are provided below.
L225: In the safety pharmacology study in cynomolgus monkeys (Study No. GS04-072), 4 male monkeys were assigned to a single treatment group. The animals were treated with vehicle control, 5 mg/kg, 10 mg/kg, and 15 mg/kg of imetelstat from Days 1- 28 via IV infusion. Each animal received a dose of vehicle control on Day 1 and increasing doses of imetelstat on Days 2, 9, 21 and 28. The results showed that single IV doses of imetelstat up to 15 mg/kg were well tolerated by cynomolgus monkeys, with no treatment-related clinical signs or changes in mean arterial pressure, heart rate, body temperature, ECG, neurological condition, blood gas parameters or chemistry and hematology parameters. Consistent with these findings, the ECG assessments evaluated in the 9-month toxicology study in monkeys were considered qualitatively and quantitatively normal and no test article-related changes were observed.
L226: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L227: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L228: ## *Single Dose Pharmacokinetics*
L229: Imetelstat is administered as an intravenous (IV) infusion, so absorption is not necessary for the drug to reach systemic circulation. In the chronic toxicology studies in mice and monkeys (Studies 003-TOX-2008 and 002-TOX-2008, respectively) and embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) toxicity studies in mice and rabbits (Studies 006-TOX-2008 and 007-TOX-2008, respectively), imetelstat was quantified in the plasma of animals using a validated competitive nucleic acid hybridization enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay. In these studies, imetelstat plasma
L230: exposures on Day 1 increased in a dose-dependent manner under the administered dosing schedules (i.e., 5 to 20 mg/kg). In mice, exposure levels increased in a linear manner, without any significant differences in values measured in male and female mice. In rabbits and monkeys, increases in plasma exposure to imetelstat (C_{max} and area under the plasma concentration-time curve from time 0 to infinity [AUC_{0-\infty}] values) were greater than dose proportional, suggesting a possible nonlinear clearance mechanism in those species. In addition, in the chronic toxicology study in monkeys, systemic exposure to imetelstat (AUC_{0-\infty}) was higher for male than for female monkeys at all dose levels (by approximately 1.2- to 1.4-fold on Day 1; however, statistical analysis of these differences was not conducted). Across species, the time to peak plasma concentration (T_{max}) levels occurred at the first sampling timepoint at 0.08 hours following dosing, and the plasma t_{1/2} of imetelstat on Day 1 ranged from a few hours (approximately 2 to 6 hours) in mice to up to 13.2 hours in monkeys, which suggests an interspecies difference in clearance, possibly due to differences in metabolic rate.
L231: ## *Distribution*
L232: Distribution studies demonstrated that in vitro plasma protein binding of imetelstat was high (&gt; 90%) across species (human, monkey, rat, and mouse) and was independent of test concentration (from 10 to 100 \muM) (Study GP05-007). Tissue distribution after IV administration of [^{35}S]-imetelstat to rats of 1 and 5 mg/kg was wide, with the highest concentrations of radioactivity seen in the liver and kidney, followed by bone marrow and spleen (Studies [b] (4)-493005 and GS03-020). In addition, a xenograft mouse study using a human OVCA-5 cell line designed to evaluate distribution of imetelstat to tumor and bone marrow demonstrated rapid distribution of imetelstat into those tissues (Study P0006-2010).
L233: ## *Metabolism and Excretion*
L234: Imetelstat is likely metabolized into smaller fragments by nucleases in tissues and the component fragments (metabolites) eliminated in the urine as the primary route (Gaus, 1997; Griffey, 1997; Crooke, 2000; Geary, 2001; Yu, 2007; Geary, 2009). Following IV administration of [^{35}S]-imetelstat to rats at 1 and 5 mg/kg, approximately 62% to 82% of the dose was recovered in the urine by 168 hours post-dose and the fecal recovery accounted for approximately 12% to 21% of the dose (Studies [b] (4)-493005 and GS03-020). In a separate study ([b] (4)-493007, [b] (4)-493007 Supplemental), the results of high performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) with ultra-violet (UV), radiometric and mass spectrometry (MS) detection indicated that intact imetelstat was not detected in urine.
L235: ## *Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions*
L236: The assessment of imetelstat inhibition of transporters, and inhibition and induction of drug metabolizing enzymes, has been performed in vitro using human biomaterials and those studies are summarized in Section 6.3.2.4 below. In an in vivo study, imetelstat did not affect the
L237: pharmacokinetics (PK) of a known Cytochrome P450 (CYP) 2C8 (CYP2C8) substrate in the rat following IV co-administration (Study (b) (4)-493006).
L238: A summary of key absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (ADME)/PK characteristics most relevant to interpretation of pharmacology/toxicology information is provided in Applicant Table 4.

L268: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L269: The FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's assessment. The Applicant stated that the margin for the embryo-lethality effect for the mouse is 2.5 times the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose. The embryo-lethality was observed in the DRF study in female mice (Study No. 004-TOX-2008) with an AUC0-last value of 342.9 hr•µg/mL at 30 mg/kg on Day 12. This results in a margin of approximately 0.6 times the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose; however, the Applicant stated that the TK samples from the DRF study were only collected at 5 minutes and 1-hour post-dose in that study, thus reflecting a truncated PK profile. As such, the AUC value of 1374.32 µg.h/mL from the 28.2 mg/kg group in the definitive EFD study in mice (Study 006-TOX-2008) was used in the safety margin calculation. The values from the TK data in the general toxicology study in mice also showed similar exposure values as in the definitive EFD study in mice, supporting the use of the exposure values from the definitive EFD study for the margin.

L272: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L273: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L274: ## The Applicant's Position:
L275: A comprehensive program of nonclinical toxicology studies was conducted with imetelstat that fulfilled all relevant International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use (ICH) guidelines for the development of a drug to treat a life-threatening condition, including guidelines S9, S1A, and S4 (ICH S1A, 1995; ICH S4, 1998; ICH S9, 2009). In addition, the imetelstat development program is consistent with the industry standards for the development of oligonucleotide (ODN) drugs (Berman, 2014; Cavagnaro, 2014). Pivotal studies were conducted according to Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) (Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development Principles of GLP).
L276: The mouse and monkey were used for the single- and repeat-dose toxicity studies, and mouse and rabbit were used for the EFD toxicity studies. These species were selected based on sequence homology to the template region of the RNA component of human telomerase and expected relevant pharmacologic action of imetelstat across species, from human to monkey to rabbit to rodent, as well as species-specific sensitivities to known ODN class effects.
L277: The general toxicities observed in rodents and/or non-rodents with imetelstat dosing were cytopenias (most notably thrombocytopenia), complement activation, and coagulation abnormalities. Complement activation and coagulation abnormalities were related to the plasma concentration of imetelstat and reflect the well-characterized toxicity profile of the related phosphorothioate ODN class. Such effects were most pronounced in the initial toxicity studies that tested very high doses of imetelstat ranging from 5 to 1230 mg/kg in mice and from 5 to 130 mg/kg in monkeys (by bolus IV injection and IV infusion) and demonstrated that the high IV doses (ie, \ge 73.8 mg/kg) produced excessive imetelstat exposures that were poorly tolerated. As demonstrated in the 9-month toxicity study in monkeys (002-TOX-2008), the degree and potential adverse sequelae of complement activation and coagulation abnormalities can be mitigated by administering imetelstat over a 2-hour IV infusion, even at doses (15 mg/kg/week) that produced exposures (C_{max}) in animals that are 6-7 x higher than the C_{max} in MDS patients at the proposed clinical dose. Mild to moderate decreases in platelet counts were seen at 10 and 15 mg/kg/week doses in the 9-month toxicity study in monkeys.
L278: Additional imetelstat toxicities observed in the chronic pivotal toxicity studies, ie, the 6-month study in mice (003-TOX-2008) and the 9-month study in monkeys, included microscopic findings in the liver and kidney (described in Applicant Table 5 and Applicant Table 6 below). Importantly, there were no degenerative changes or alterations in clinical pathology parameters indicative of hepatic dysfunction or injury (ie, no substantial elevations in serum
L279: transaminases, bilirubin, or alkaline phosphatase), nor were there any remarkable changes in parameters of kidney function.
L280: The toxicity studies most relevant to the intended clinical use of imetelstat based on duration of treatment in both species, as well as the mode of administration employed (ie, 2-hour IV infusion in monkeys), are the chronic pivotal toxicity studies in mice and monkeys. Findings from these studies are summarized in detail in the tabulated summaries below in Applicant Table 5 and Applicant Table 6.
L281: ## **Applicant Table 5: Tabulated Summary of Data from Chronic Pivotal Toxicology Study of Imetelstat in Mice**
L282: Study title / study number / electronic Common Technical Document (eCTD) location: GRN163L – A 6-month intravenous toxicity study in mice with an 8-week recovery period / 003-TOX-2008 / 4.2.3.2
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Discoloration at the injection site (tail vein), with dose-dependent severity at \ge 5 mg/kg/day, which correlated with microscopic observations of necrosis/inflammation and vascular/perivascular fibrosis at the injection site of the 20 mg/kg animals.
Mild decreases in RBC parameters (hematocrit, Hgb, and RBC counts) observed at \ge 15 mg/kg in both sexes, and these findings correlated with extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen at 20 mg/kg and increased spleen weights (at all dose levels).
Changes in organ weights included increases in liver weights at \ge 15 mg/kg in both sexes and increased kidney weights in males at 20 mg/kg.
Microscopic findings in the liver consisted of increases in mixed inflammatory foci and increases in the number of Kupffer cells (hyperplasia of this cell type) and/or histiocytic aggregates containing brown and/or blue pigment in the liver.
Most systemic effects showed evidence of recovery after the 8-week treatment-free period. Increases in spleen weight at \ge 5 mg/kg and in liver weight at 20 mg/kg in males were considered to be partially reversible.
No-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) for systemic toxicity: 20 mg/kg
NOAEL for local (tail vein injection site) toxicity: 5 mg/kg

GLP compliance: Yes

Age:	13 weeks

L295: ## **Applicant Table 6: Tabulated Summary of Data from Chronic Pivotal Toxicology Study of Imetelstat in Cynomolgus Monkeys**
L296: Study title / study number / eCTD location: A 9-month Toxicity Study of GRN163L in Cynomolgus Monkeys Followed by an 14-week Recovery Period / 002-TOX-2008 / 4.2.3.2
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
Mild decreases in RBC parameters at \ge 5 mg/kg.
Evidence of erythroid hyperplasia at 15 mg/kg based on evaluation of bone marrow smears.
Platelet counts were highly variable but were mildly lower at 10 and 15 mg/kg.
Moderate prolongation of activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) acutely following dosing (when measured on Day 239).
Initial activation of complement that was immediate and driven by the alternative pathway (Bb). For longer-term treatment beyond 6 months, the Bb diminished but was still significantly high. Chronic treatment caused depletion of classical pathway activity (CH50), indicating consumption of complement factors stemming from activation. 10 and 15 mg/kg/week dose levels produced a biologically significant degree of complement activation, based on increases in C5a. During the recovery period, the values for all complement parameters remained only slightly elevated over baseline and showed reversal.
Increased liver and kidney weights, with microscopic correlates of mild to moderate changes in the kidney (mesangial thickening, glomerulonephritis/sclerosis, deposition in interstitium, renal tubular
L297: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217779 imetelstat
## TRADENAME, imetelstat
L298: hemorrhage, protein casts, and regeneration) and in the liver (telangiectasis, brown pigment deposition in Kupffer cells).
Changes also noted in the uterus (atrophy of the endometrium), choroid plexus (minimal lymphoid follicle formation and mononuclear cellular infiltration), and general systemic arteritis, including at the injection site.
Most changes showed evidence of recovery after a 14-week drug-free period. Test article-related changes that persisted through the end of the recovery period in high-dose (15 mg/kg/) animals were generally diminished in severity and included increased kidney weights, decreased indices of red cell mass, enlarged spleen, discoloration of the liver and kidney, and microscopic changes in the kidneys, liver, and choroid plexus.
NOAEL: not determined; Highest Non-Severely Toxic Dose Level: 15 mg/kg

L308: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L309: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment for the general toxicology studies. The review below provides a summary for the 8-week repeat-dose studies in mice and monkeys conducted by the Applicant.
L310: In the 8-week GLP repeat-dose toxicity study in mice with a 4-week recovery period (Study No. GS04-070), the animals were administered imetelstat at doses of 5, 15, or 30 mg/kg BIW via IV injection. There was a total of 20 early deaths or moribund sacrifices during the study, mainly occurring at the HD (30 mg/kg): 14 males and 2 females in the HD group, 1 male in the MD group (15 mg/kg), and 3 males in the LD (5 mg/kg) group. There were no clinical signs that preceded the deaths, and the cause of the deaths was not determined. Decreases in platelet and lymphocyte counts were observed at \ge 15 mg/kg. Increased blood urea nitrogen (BUN) and decreased phosphorus levels were observed in males at \ge 15 mg/kg. Increases in liver and
L311: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L312: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L313: spleen weights were observed at \ge 15 mg/kg, which correlated with microscopic findings in these organs. Notable microscopic findings included neutrophil infiltrate and increased extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen, extramedullary hematopoiesis in the liver, and minimal mineralization of myocytes in the heart of males. Additional findings of minimal to mild lymphoid atrophy in the spleen and thymus were also observed. An increase in the severity of hemorrhage at the injection sites in the tail veins of the mice was observed at \ge 15 mg/kg. Imetelstat-related effects were reversible by the end of the recovery period.
L314: The 8-week GLP repeat-dose study in monkeys with a 4-week recovery period evaluated imetelstat doses of vehicle control (2 groups for infusion rates), 5, 10, 15, and 20 mg/kg administered once weekly via IV infusion (vehicle control and 20 mg/kg groups were infused over 24 hours; the other vehicle control, 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg groups were infused over 6 hours; Study No. GS04-071). There was no unscheduled mortality in any of the treatment groups. A dose-dependent prolongation of aPTT was observed but moderated in the 20 mg/kg with 24-hour infusion group. Increases in plasma Bb indicative of activation of the alternative complement pathway were observed at \ge 10 mg/kg. No effects on CH50 (total hemolytic complement) or C5a levels were noted. Increases in BUN and creatinine were noted at 20 mg/kg, reflecting an effect on renal function that correlated with increased kidney weight and proteinuria. Decreases in albumin and increases in globulin levels were noted at \ge 15 mg/kg. Microscopic findings included mesangial thickening and glomerulonephritis in the kidney, cellular infiltration in the liver, mononuclear cell infiltration in the brain, and thickened catheter tip site. There were inflammation, thrombosis, proliferation, hemorrhage, and necrosis observed microscopically in the area of the infusion site. The changes in brain, kidney and tissues surrounding the IV catheter tip remained after a 4-week recovery period.
L315: ## 5.5.2. Genotoxicity
L316: ## The Applicant's Position:
L317: In a standard battery of genotoxicity studies (summarized in Applicant Table 7), imetelstat was not mutagenic in an in vitro bacterial mutagenicity assay (Ames test) or clastogenic in an in vitro chromosomal aberrations assay (using cultured human peripheral blood lymphocytes) or in a standard mouse in vivo micronucleus assay.

L324: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L325: The FDA concurs with the Applicant's assessment. The review below provides additional information for the methods and data presented by the Applicant.
L326: In the initial chromosomal aberration study, human peripheral blood lymphocytes were incubated with imetelstat at 33.9 – 5000 µg/mL for 3 hours with and without metabolic activation and harvested 22 hours after the initial treatment. Cells treated with concentrations up to 2450 µg/mL without metabolic activation and up to 1200 µg/mL with metabolic activation were analyzed for chromosomal aberrations. In the confirmatory assay, human peripheral blood lymphocytes were incubated with imetelstat at 37.5 – 2500 µg/mL without metabolic activation for 22 hours and 150 µg/mL – 2000 µg/mL with metabolic activation for 3 hours. Cells treated with concentrations up to 800 µg/mL without metabolic activation and up to 100 µg/mL with metabolic activation were analyzed for chromosomal aberrations. No statistically significant increases in cells with chromosomal aberrations, polyploidy, or endoreduplication were observed.
L327: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L328: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L329: In alignment with ICH S9 guidance, carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with imetelstat based on its intended clinical use for treatment of transfusion-dependent anemic patients with MDS who have a limited life-expectancy.
L330: Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L331: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L332: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L333: The FDA disagrees with the Applicant that no carcinogenicity risk assessment is warranted for the intended patient population, which includes patients with low-risk MDS. The need for carcinogenicity studies depends on the life expectancy of the patients for the proposed indications. FDA asked the Applicant to provide justification for not conducting carcinogenicity studies based on the life expectancy of low-risk MDS. Based on the literature provided by the Applicant and discussion with the clinical team, the life expectancy for patients with lower risk MDS (defined variously, but usually as low or intermediate-1 risk disease by IPSS) and ESA failure or transfusion dependency can be &gt; 5 years and carcinogenicity risk assessment is warranted for the intended patient population. To address the carcinogenicity risk assessment of imetelstat, there will be separate Post Marketing Requirements (PMRs) for carcinogenicity assessments in the mouse and rat species.
L334: ## 5.5.4. **Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology**
L335: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L336: ## *Fertility, Pre- and Post-Natal Development*
L337: Consistent with ICH S9 guidelines, standalone fertility, pre- and post-natal development studies were not conducted based on imetelstat's intended clinical use for treatment of transfusion-dependent anemic patients with MDS with limited life-expectancy. However, information from general chronic toxicology studies informed assessments of potential impairment of fertility. In the chronic toxicity study in monkeys where imetelstat was administered once weekly for 9 months, uterine endometrial atrophy was noted at the high dose of 14.1 mg/kg (equivalent to 15 mg/kg imetelstat sodium), reflecting a mean exposure (based on AUC) that is approximately 14.5-times the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose. This finding was reversible after a 14-week recovery period. There were no gross or histological changes for the male reproductive tissues noted at any dose tested, i.e., up 18.8 mg/kg (equivalent to 20 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) in the 6-month toxicity study in mice or 14.1 mg/kg (equivalent to 15 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) in the 9-month toxicity study in monkeys, reflecting mean exposures (based on AUC) that are 1.7-times and 20.5-times, respectively, the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose (Studies 003-TOX-2008 and 002-TOX-2008, respectively).
L338: ## *Embryo-Fetal Development*
L339: In EFD toxicity studies (refer to Applicant Table 8 and Applicant Table 9), imetelstat was not teratogenic, and there was no evidence of any fetal external malformations in either species. Embryo-lethal effects were observed at 28.2 mg/kg (equivalent to 30 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) in both species, noted as increased post-implantation loss due to an increase in early resorptions, resulting in a decrease in viable fetuses and litter size per animal. These effects
L340: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217779 imetelstat
## TRADENAME, imetelstat
L341: were observed at a dose that produced exposures (based on AUC) that are approximately 2.5-times (mice) or 19.2-times (rabbits) the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose.
L342: In mice, there were no maternal or developmental toxicities observed at the doses tested in the pivotal EFD study (up to 28.2 mg/kg/dose; Study 006-TOX-2008; Applicant Table 8). However, embryo-lethality was observed at 28.2 mg/kg/dose in the dose range-finding EFD study in mice (refer to Applicant Table 9; Study 004-TOX-2008), and those results are taken into consideration in Sponsor's assessment of risk of embryo-lethality included in the proposed product label.
L343: In the pivotal EFD study in rabbits (007-TOX-2008; Applicant Table 8), doses of 14.1 and 28.2 mg/kg (equivalent to 15 and 30 mg/kg imetelstat sodium) were considered to be maternally toxic based on a decrease in mean gestation body weight, such effects occurring at exposures that are 9.4- to 19.2 times, respectively, the human exposure at the recommended clinical dose.
